*   Paths to F: H-G-F-E, H-X-G-F-E, H-X-K-F-E. These paths are unaffected by removing J or C. Thus, H and E are still connected.

This systematic check demonstrates that any combination of two nodes will leave at least one of the three main "channels" (via M, B, or F) open, or fail to cut all paths within a channel while leaving others open. Therefore, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

**Attempting a cut set of size 3:**
If I remove the set of nodes {M, B, F}:
*   Removing M disconnects all paths that use M to reach E.
*   Removing B disconnects all paths that use B to reach E.
*   Removing F disconnects all paths that use F to reach E.

Since every path from H to E must pass through at least one of these three nodes, removing all three of them will guarantee that no path remains between H and E.

Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

2.  **Identify Neighbors:**
    *   Neighbors of E: F, L, M, H, B
    *   Neighbors of I: H, B, C, J, P
3.  **Explore Paths and Identify Bottlenecks:** I need to find all distinct paths between E and I. To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths. If I can find *k* vertex-disjoint paths, then I know that at least *k* nodes must be removed to disconnect the target nodes (by Menger's Theorem).

    Let's try to find vertex-disjoint paths (excluding E and I themselves):
    *   **Path 1:** E - H - I
        *   Intermediate node: {H}
    *   **Path 2:** E - B - C - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (H is not B or C).
    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (M, J are not H) and Path 2 (M, J are not B, C).

    Since I have found three vertex-disjoint paths (E-H-I, E-B-C-I, E-M-J-I), it means that any set of nodes that disconnects E and I must contain at least one node from each of these paths (excluding E and I). Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

4.  **Construct a Cut Set of Size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects E and I. Based on the vertex-disjoint paths identified, I can try to pick one node from each path's intermediate nodes.

    Let's consider the cut set: **[H, B, J]**
    *   Removing H breaks Path 1 (E-H-I) and any other path going through H (e.g., E-F-G-H-I).
    *   Removing B breaks Path 2 (E-B-C-I) and any other path going through B (e.g., E-B-I, E-B-N-O-P-I).
    *   Removing J breaks Path 3 (E-M-J-I) and any other path going through J (e.g., E-L-M-J-I, E-M-J-P-I).

5.  **Verify the Cut Set [H, B, J]:**
    Let's check all possible ways to get from E to I after removing H, B, and J.
    Any path from E to I must start with one of E's neighbors: F, L, M, H, B.
    *   **E -> H:** H is removed. This branch is cut.
    *   **E -> B:** B is removed. This branch is cut.
    *   **E -> M:** M is connected to J. J is removed. So, E-M-J-... paths are cut. M is also connected to L. E-M-L-... paths are not directly to I.
    *   **E -> L:** L is connected to M. M is connected to J. J is removed. So, E-L-M-J-... paths are cut.
    *   **E -> F:** F is connected to G. G is connected to H. H is removed. So, E-F-G-H-... paths are cut. F is also connected to K and X, but these do not lead to I without passing through H or B or J.

    Since all initial branches from E (via H, B, M, L, F) are effectively severed from reaching I due to the removal of H, B, and J, the two black nodes E and I are completely disconnected.

6.  **Conclusion:** We found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, implying a minimum cut of at least 3. We then found a cut set of size 3 ([H, B, J]) that successfully disconnects E and I. Therefore, 3 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are M and B. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect M and B. I cannot remove M or B.

2.  **Analyze Connectivity from M:**
    *   Node M is directly connected to E, L, and J.
    *   Node L is only connected to M and E. This means any path from M that goes through L must also pass through E (M-L-E-...).
    *   Therefore, all paths from M to B must pass through either node E or node J. This implies that any cut set must contain at least one node from the "E-branch" and at least one node from the "J-branch" (or a node that is common to all paths, which would be a single point of failure).

3.  **Check for a 1-node cut:**
    *   If I remove E: Paths M-E-B, M-L-E-B, M-E-F-...-B are broken. However, paths starting with M-J-...-B still exist (e.g., M-J-O-N-B). So, E alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove J: Paths M-J-...-B are broken. However, paths starting with M-E-...-B still exist (e.g., M-E-B). So, J alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove any other single node (e.g., N, C, H, P, O, I, F, K, G, X, D, Y), there will always be paths remaining through E or J. For example, removing N leaves M-E-B and M-J-P-C-B.
    *   Conclusion: A single node removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.

4.  **Check for a 2-node cut:**
    *   Based on step 2, we need to cut both the "E-branch" and the "J-branch".
    *   **Cutting the E-branch:** The most efficient way to cut all paths from M through E (including M-L-E-...) is to remove node E itself. This breaks all paths M-E-...-B.
    *   **Cutting the J-branch:** Now, assuming E is removed, we need to disconnect J from B. Let's analyze paths from J to B.
        *   J is connected to D, O, P.
        *   D is only connected to P. So any path J-D-... must go through P (J-D-P-...).
        *   Therefore, all paths from J to B must pass through P or O.
        *   Let's test if removing P or O alone disconnects J from B:
            *   If I remove P: Paths J-D-P-...-B and J-P-...-B are broken. Remaining paths from J are J-O-N-B and J-O-Y-N-B (since O's other connection is to P, which is removed). Both of these remaining paths go through N. So, if P is removed, N becomes a single point of failure for the J-O paths.
            *   If I remove O: Paths J-O-N-B, J-O-Y-N-B, and J-O-P-...-B are broken. Remaining paths from J are J-D-P-...-B and J-P-...-B. These paths can go through C or I to B (e.g., J-D-P-C-B, J-D-P-I-H-B). These are independent paths, so removing O alone is not enough.
        *   This means that to disconnect J from B, we need to remove at least two nodes from the J-branch. For example, removing P and N would disconnect J from B (as shown above, after P is removed, N is the only way out for the remaining J-O paths). Another option is removing P and C, or P and H, or O and C, O and H, etc. However, the combination {P, O} is a direct cut for all paths from J, as all paths from J must go through P or O (since D only leads to P).

5.  **Evaluate 2-node cut possibilities:**
    *   If we pick E (1 node for E-branch), we need at least 2 nodes for the J-branch (e.g., {P, O} or {P, N}). This would result in a total of 1 + 2 = 3 nodes.
    *   This suggests that a 2-node cut might not be possible. Let's systematically check pairs of nodes that could potentially disconnect M and B.
    *   Consider the direct neighbors of B: {E, N, C, H}.
        *   Removing {E, N}: Paths like M-J-P-C-B and M-J-D-P-I-H-B still exist.
        *   Removing {E, C}: Paths like M-J-O-N-B and M-J-D-P-I-H-B still exist.

        *   Removing {N, H}: Paths like M-E-B and M-J-P-C-B still exist.
        *   Removing {C, H}: Paths like M-E-B and M-J-O-N-B still exist.
    *   Since none of these 2-node combinations disconnect M and B, and we've established that a 1-node cut is impossible, the minimum number of nodes must be 3.

6.  **Confirm a 3-node cut:**
    *   As identified in step 4, removing E disconnects the E-branch.
    *   Removing P and O disconnects the J-branch (because J connects to D, O, P; D connects only to P; so all paths from J must go through P or O).
    *   Therefore, the set {E, P, O} will disconnect M and B.
    *   Another valid 3-node cut is {E, P, N}. If E is removed, all M-E paths are gone. If P is removed, all M-J-D-P and M-J-P paths are gone. The only remaining paths from J are M-J-O-N-B and M-J-O-Y-N-B. Removing N breaks both of these. So {E, P, N} is also a valid cut set of size 3.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between G and I. This is a min-cut problem. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

First, I will try to identify edge-disjoint paths from G to I.

1.  **Path 1:** G - H - I
    Edges in Path 1: `{(G, H), (H, I)}`

2.  Now, I look for a path from G to I that does not use any edges from Path 1.
    **Path 2:** G - F - E - B - C - I
    Edges in Path 2: `{(G, F), (F, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, I)}`
    Path 2 is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

3.  Next, I try to find a third path from G to I that is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.
    Edges used so far: `{(G, H), (H, I), (G, F), (F, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, I)}`

    From G, the only remaining outgoing edge not used in Path 1 or Path 2 is `(G, X)`. So, any third edge-disjoint path must start with `G - X`.
    From X, there are two options: `(X, K)` or `(X, F)`.

    *   **Option 3a:** `G - X - K`
        From K, the only outgoing edge not used is `(K, F)`.
        So, `G - X - K - F`.
        From F, the outgoing edges are `(F, E)`, `(F, G)`, `(F, X)`, `(F, K)`.
        `(F, E)` is used in Path 2.
        `(F, G)` is used in Path 2.
        `(F, X)` is part of the current path `G-X-K-F`.
        `(F, K)` is part of the current path `G-X-K-F`.
        Since `(F, E)` is used by Path 2, this branch `G - X - K - F` cannot proceed to I without using an edge already in Path 1 or Path 2.

    *   **Option 3b:** `G - X - F`
        From F, the outgoing edges are `(F, E)`, `(F, G)`, `(F, X)`, `(F, K)`.
        `(F, E)` is used in Path 2.
        `(F, G)` is used in Path 2.
        `(F, X)` is part of the current path `G-X-F`.
        `(F, K)` is not used.
        So, `G - X - F - K`.
        From K, the outgoing edges are `(K, X)`, `(K, F)`.
        `(K, X)` is part of the current path `G-X-F-K`.
        `(K, F)` is part of the current path `G-X-F-K`.
        This branch `G - X - F - K` also cannot proceed to I without using an edge already in Path 1 or Path 2 (specifically, it would need to use `(F,E)` to get to the right side of the graph).

Since I cannot find a third edge-disjoint path, it implies that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between G and I is 2. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect G and I is 2.

Now, I need to identify a cut set of 2 edges. I can do this by finding a partition of the graph into two sets, S and T, such that G is in S and I is in T, and there are exactly two edges connecting S and T.

Let's define the set S as `{G, X, K, F}`. These are the nodes that are "closely connected" to G without passing through a potential cut edge.
Let the set T be all other nodes: `{H, I, B, C, N, J, P, D, O, Y, E, M, L}`.

Now, I identify all edges that connect a node in S to a node in T:
1.  `(G, H)`: G is in S, H is in T.
2.  `(F, E)`: F is in S, E is in T.

All other edges within S (e.g., (G,X), (G,F), (X,K), (X,F), (K,F)) do not cross this partition.
All other edges within T (e.g., (H,I), (E,B), (B,C), (C,I), (M,J), etc.) do not cross this partition.

If I remove the edges `(G, H)` and `(F, E)`, then G (and its connected component {G, X, K, F}) will be completely disconnected from I (and the rest of the graph).

Let's verify that removing these two edges breaks all paths:
*   Any path starting `G - H - ...` is broken by removing `(G, H)`.
*   Any path starting `G - F - E - ...` is broken by removing `(F, E)`.
*   Any path starting `G - X - F - E - ...` is broken by removing `(F, E)`.
*   Any path starting `G - X - K - F - E - ...` is broken by removing `(F, E)`.

All possible paths from G to I must use either `(G, H)` or pass through `F` and then `E`. Therefore, removing `(G, H)` and `(F, E)` will disconnect G from I.

    *   Intermediate node: {M}

    *   Intermediate nodes: {F, K, G, H, I}
    *   (Note: There are variations like E-F-X-K... but they all share F, K, G, H, I or a subset of these, and are distinct from Path 1 and Path 3).

3.  **Path 3: E - B - C - O - P - J**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C, O, P}
    *   (Note: There are variations like E-B-N-Y-O-P-J, E-B-N-C-O-P-J, E-B-C-I-J, E-B-H-I-J, but this path is chosen to be disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2).

Let's check if the sets of intermediate nodes for these three paths are disjoint:
*   Set 1: {M}
*   Set 2: {F, K, G, H, I}
*   Set 3: {B, C, O, P}

These three sets are indeed mutually disjoint. This means there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths between E and J. Therefore, according to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect E and J is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3.
Consider the immediate neighbors of E: M, L, F, B.
Any path from E to J must pass through M, L, F, or B.
*   Paths through L eventually pass through M (E -> L -> M -> J).
*   So, all paths from E must pass through M, F, or B.

Let's propose the cut set {M, F, B}:
1.  **Removing M:** This breaks all paths that go through M, including E - M - J and E - L - M - J.
2.  **Removing F:** This breaks all paths that start with E - F, such as E - F - X - K - G - H - I - J and E - F - K - G - H - I - J.
3.  **Removing B:** This breaks all paths that start with E - B, such as E - B - N - Y - O - P - J, E - B - N - C - O - P - J, E - B - C - O - P - J, E - B - C - I - J, and E - B - H - I - J.

By removing M, F, and B, all possible paths from E to J are severed. Since we established that the minimum cut size is at least 3, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, F, B]

    *   Node D is connected to nodes J and P.

4.  **Evaluate Cut Set [(J, D), (P, D)]:**

    *   If edge (P, D) is removed, any path from F to D that uses P is broken.
    *   Since D has no other connections, removing both (J, D) and (P, D) ensures that D is completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore from F.
    *   This cut set has a size of 2.
5.  **Check for Smaller Cut:**
    *   Could we disconnect F and D by removing only one edge?
    *   If we remove (J, D), a path like F-E-I-P-D still exists.
    *   If we remove (P, D), a path like F-E-I-J-D still exists.
    *   If we remove an edge connected to F, for example (F, E), paths through G still exist (e.g., F-G-H-I-J-D).
    *   Therefore, a single edge removal is not sufficient to disconnect F and D.
6.  **Conclusion:** Since a cut of size 1 is not possible, and we have found a valid cut of size 2, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

An alternative minimal cut set of size 2 would be [(F, E), (G, H)].
*   Removing (F, E) breaks all paths from F that go through E.
*   Removing (G, H) breaks all paths from F that go through G (since G's only connection to the "D-side" of the graph is H; X and K only connect to F and G).

    *   Node B is connected to H, C, N.
    *   Node M is connected to E, L, J.

2.  **Consider a simple strategy: Isolate one of the target nodes.**
    If we remove all edges directly connected to M, M will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from B.
    The edges connected to M are (E, M), (L, M), and (J, M).
    Removing these 3 edges would disconnect M from the graph. This gives a cut set of size 3: `[(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)]`.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., 1 or 2 edges) is possible.**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and M. If I can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to cut is at least 3.

    Let's find edge-disjoint paths from B to M:
    *   **Path 1:** B - H - E - M
        Edges: `(B, H)`, `(H, E)`, `(E, M)`

        B - C - I - J - M
        Edges: `(B, C)`, `(C, I)`, `(I, J)`, `(J, M)`
        This path uses entirely different edges than Path 1. So, Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3:** Now, I look for a third path from B to M that does not use any of the edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
        Edges used so far:

        From Path 2: `(B, C)`, `(C, I)`, `(I, J)`, `(J, M)`

        From B, the only remaining unused outgoing edge is `(B, N)`.

        B - N - O - P - I - H - G - E - L - M

        ` (B, N)` - Unused.
        ` (N, O)` - Unused.
        ` (O, P)` - Unused.
        ` (P, I)` - Unused (P2 used `(C, I)` and `(I, J)`).
        ` (I, H)` - Unused (P1 used `(B, H)` and `(H, E)`).
        ` (H, G)` - Unused.
        ` (G, E)` - Unused (P1 used `(H, E)`).
        ` (E, L)` - Unused (P1 used `(E, M)`).
        ` (L, M)` - Unused.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H (black) and E (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between H and E.

First, I identify all possible paths from H to E. I can categorize these paths based on the first edge taken from H and the last edge taken to E.

Edges directly connected to H are: (H, I), (H, X), (H, G).
Edges directly connected to E are: (B, E), (F, E), (L, E).

Let's list the main path structures:

**Paths starting with (H, I):**
1.  H-I-C-B-E (This path uses (B, E) to reach E)
2.  H-I-C-N-B-E (Also uses (B, E))
3.  H-I-C-N-O-B-E (Also uses (B, E))
4.  H-I-C-P-O-B-E (Also uses (B, E))
5.  H-I-J-M-L-E (This path uses (L, E) to reach E)

**Paths starting with (H, X):**
1.  H-X-K-F-E (This path uses (F, E) to reach E)
2.  H-X-G-F-E (Also uses (F, E))
3.  H-X-G-K-F-E (Also uses (F, E))

**Paths starting with (H, G):**
1.  H-G-F-E (This path uses (F, E) to reach E)
2.  H-G-K-F-E (Also uses (F, E))
3.  H-G-X-K-F-E (Also uses (F, E))

Now, I look for a minimal set of edges that can cut all these paths.

**Strategy: Identify bottleneck edges.**

1.  **Observation 1:** All paths starting with (H, X) or (H, G) eventually lead to node F, and then use the edge (F, E) to reach E.
    *   From H-X: You can go to K or G. Both K and G are connected to F (K-F, G-F). So, any path from H-X to E must pass through F and then (F, E).
    *   From H-G: You can go to F, K, or X. F directly connects to E via (F, E). K connects to F (K-F). X connects to K or G, which then lead to F. So, any path from H-G to E must pass through F and then (F, E).
    *   Therefore, removing the edge **(F, E)** will disconnect all paths originating from H via (H, X) or (H, G).

2.  **After removing (F, E):** The only remaining paths from H to E are those that started with (H, I). These are:
    *   H-I-C-B-E
    *   H-I-C-N-B-E
    *   H-I-C-N-O-B-E
    *   H-I-C-P-O-B-E
    *   H-I-J-M-L-E

3.  **Observation 2:** All these remaining paths share the initial edge **(H, I)**. If we remove (H, I), all these paths will be broken.

4.  **Proposed Cut Set:** Based on these observations, removing the edges **(F, E)** and **(H, I)** should disconnect H and E. This set has 2 edges.

**Verification:**
*   If (H, I) is removed, no path can start from H via I. This breaks all paths in the "Paths starting with (H, I)" group.
*   If (F, E) is removed, no path can reach E via F. As established in Observation 1, all paths starting from H via (H, X) or (H, G) must pass through F to reach E. Thus, removing (F, E) breaks all paths in the "Paths starting with (H, X)" and "Paths starting with (H, G)" groups.

Since all possible paths from H to E are covered by these two removals, H and E will be completely disconnected.

**Minimality Check:**
*   If only one edge is removed (e.g., (H, I)), paths like H-X-K-F-E would still exist.
*   If only one edge is removed (e.g., (F, E)), paths like H-I-C-B-E or H-I-J-M-L-E would still exist.
Therefore, a single edge is not sufficient, and 2 is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(H, I), (F, E)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B and M. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between B and M.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node B is connected to E, H, N, C.
    *   Node M is connected to L, F.

2.  **Analyze paths from B to M:**
    Any path from B to M must eventually reach one of M's direct neighbors (L or F) and then use the edge connecting that neighbor to M.

    Let's list the paths from B to L and F:
    *   **Paths from B to L:**
        *   B -> E -> L
    *   **Paths from B to F:**
        *   B -> E -> F
        *   B -> H -> G -> F
        *   B -> H -> G -> K -> F
        *   B -> H -> G -> X -> F

    Notice that the nodes N, C, I, J, D, P, O, Y form a separate part of the graph that does not connect to L or F. Therefore, any path from B to M must pass through E or H, and then eventually through L or F.

3.  **Consider the edges directly connected to M:**
    Node M has only two edges connecting it to the rest of the graph: (L, M) and (F, M).
    *   If we remove the edge (L, M), any path that uses L to reach M (e.g., B -> E -> L -> M) will be broken.
    *   If we remove the edge (F, M), any path that uses F to reach M (e.g., B -> E -> F -> M, B -> H -> G -> F -> M, etc.) will be broken.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut set:**
    *   To completely disconnect M from the rest of the graph (and thus from B), both of its incoming/outgoing edges must be removed.
    *   If we remove only one edge, for example (L, M), then paths like B -> E -> F -> M would still exist.
    *   If we remove only one edge, for example (F, M), then paths like B -> E -> L -> M would still exist.
    *   Therefore, removing a single edge is not sufficient to disconnect B and M.
    *   Removing both edges (L, M) and (F, M) will isolate M, ensuring no path exists between B and M. This set has 2 edges.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    The minimum number of edges to remove is 2. The cut set consists of the two edges directly connected to node M.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(L, M), (F, M)]

    *   Edges in Path 3: (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)

4.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., 1 or 2 edges) is possible:**

    *   **Path 1 (to B):** E - B
        *   Edges: `(E, B)`

2.  **Initial Path Analysis:**
    *   H is directly connected to G, X, I, B.
    *   E is directly connected to M, F, B, C.
    *   Notice that node B is directly connected to both H and E (H-B-E). Since B is a white node, it can be removed. If B is not removed, the path H-B-E will always exist, and thus H and E will remain connected. Therefore, B *must* be part of any cut set.

3.  **Assume B is Removed:**
    *   If node B is removed, the direct path H-B-E is broken.
    *   All other paths that pass through B (e.g., H-B-N-C-E, H-B-N-O-C-E, etc.) are also broken.
    *   Now, H is connected to G, X, I.
    *   E is connected to M, F, C.
    *   The problem reduces to finding the minimum number of additional nodes to remove to disconnect the set {G, X, I} from the set {M, F, C}.

4.  **Analyze Remaining Paths (after removing B):**
    Let's list the remaining paths from H's neighbors (G, X, I) to E's neighbors (M, F, C):

    *   **Paths from G to E's neighbors:**
        *   H-G-F-E (via F)
        *   H-G-K-M-E (via M)
        *   H-G-K-X-F-E (via F)

    *   **Paths from X to E's neighbors:**
        *   H-X-F-E (via F)
        *   H-X-K-M-E (via M)
        *   H-X-K-G-F-E (via F)

    *   **Paths from I to E's neighbors:**
        *   H-I-C-E (via C)
        *   H-I-P-O-C-E (via C)
        *   H-I-P-J-M-E (via M)

5.  **Determine Additional Nodes Needed:**
    To disconnect H from E, we need to ensure that no path from {G, X, I} can reach {M, F, C}.
    Let's consider the "entry points" to E's side: M, F, C.

    *   **To break all paths to E via F:** We must remove node F. (Paths like H-G-F-E, H-X-F-E, H-G-K-X-F-E, H-X-K-G-F-E are broken).
    *   **To break all paths to E via M:** We must remove node M. (Paths like H-G-K-M-E, H-X-K-M-E, H-I-P-J-M-E are broken).
    *   **To break all paths to E via C:** We must remove node C. (Paths like H-I-C-E, H-I-P-O-C-E are broken).

    If we remove F, M, and C, then E will have no remaining connections to the rest of the graph (since B is already removed).

6.  **Form the Cut Set:**
    Combining the necessary nodes: B (from step 2) and F, M, C (from step 5).
    The cut set is [B, F, M, C]. This set has 4 nodes.

7.  **Verify Minimum Size:**
    *   We established that B *must* be in the cut set.
    *   After removing B, we need to disconnect {G, X, I} from {M, F, C}.
    *   Let's check if removing any two nodes from {F, M, C} is sufficient:
        *   If we remove {F, M}: Paths H-I-C-E and H-I-P-O-C-E still exist (via C).
        *   If we remove {F, C}: Paths H-G-K-M-E, H-X-K-M-E, and H-I-P-J-M-E still exist (via M).

    *   Since removing any two nodes from {F, M, C} (in addition to B) leaves at least one path, it means we need to remove all three nodes F, M, and C to completely disconnect H from E.
    *   Therefore, a cut set of size 3 (B + 2 other nodes) is not possible.
    *   The minimum number of nodes to remove is 4.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 4, Cut Set: [B, F, M, C]

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt2_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 3
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, X and C.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are X and C.

2.  **Strategy: Find edge-disjoint paths.** According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut is at least *k*. If I then find a cut set of size *k*, that will be the minimum.

3.  **Identify paths from X to C:**
    Let's try to find distinct paths from X to C, ensuring they do not share any edges.

    *   **Path 1:** X - F - E - B - C
        Edges: (X,F), (F,E), (E,B), (B,C)

    *   **Path 2:** X - K - J - P - O - C
        Edges: (X,K), (K,J), (J,P), (P,O), (O,C)

    *   **Path 3:** X - G - H - I - C
        Edges: (X,G), (G,H), (H,I), (I,C)

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    Let's list all edges used in these three paths:
    *   From Path 1: (X,F), (F,E), (E,B), (B,C)
    *   From Path 2: (X,K), (K,J), (J,P), (P,O), (O,C)
    *   From Path 3: (X,G), (G,H), (H,I), (I,C)

    Upon inspection, all edges listed are unique to their respective paths. There are no common edges between Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3. Therefore, these three paths are edge-disjoint.

5.  **Determine the lower bound for the cut:** Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between X and C, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

6.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect a node from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to it.
    *   Node X is connected to F, K, and G.

    *   If I remove **E**: Paths like G -> H -> I still exist. So, E alone is not sufficient.
    *   If I remove any other single node (e.g., F, K, X, C, P, J, M, B, etc.), there will always be at least one path remaining through H or E. For example, removing J still leaves G-H-I and G-F-E-H-I.

Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are H and E. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H and E. I cannot remove H or E.

2.  **Explore Paths:** I will systematically list all possible paths from H to E.
    *   **Paths starting H -> G:**
        *   H - G - F - P - E
        *   H - G - K - F - P - E
        *   H - G - X - F - P - E
    *   **Paths starting H -> I:**
        *   H - I - P - E
        *   H - I - J - P - E
        *   H - I - J - M - E (This path uses M, which is a neighbor of E)
    *   **Paths starting H -> C:**
        *   H - C - B - E
        *   H - C - N - O - P - E
        *   H - C - O - P - E

3.  **Identify Bottlenecks/Critical Nodes:** To disconnect H and E, all these paths must be broken. I will look for nodes that are critical to multiple paths or represent distinct "channels" between H and E.

    Let's consider the "entry points" to E's neighborhood from H's side. The direct neighbors of E are P, L, M, B.
    *   Nodes L and M are connected to E. Can H reach L or M without passing through P or B?
        *   H -> I -> J -> M -> E (Yes, this path exists and uses M)
        *   H -> I -> J -> M -> L -> E (This path also exists and uses L and M)
    *   So, paths can reach E via P, B, or M (and L via M).

    Let's categorize the paths based on which neighbor of E they use as their last hop (excluding L, as it's only connected to M and E, so any path through L must also pass through M):
    *   **Group 1 (via P):** All paths that end with `... - P - E`.
        *   H - G - F - P - E
        *   H - G - K - F - P - E
        *   H - G - X - F - P - E
        *   H - I - P - E
        *   H - I - J - P - E
        *   H - C - N - O - P - E
        *   H - C - O - P - E
        *   Removing node **P** would break all these paths.

    *   **Group 2 (via B):** All paths that end with `... - B - E`.
        *   H - C - B - E
        *   Removing node **B** would break this path.

    *   **Group 3 (via M):** All paths that end with `... - M - E`.
        *   H - I - J - M - E
        *   H - I - J - M - L - E (This path also uses M)
        *   Removing node **M** (or J or I) would break these paths.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Vertex-Disjoint Paths):** To find the minimum number of nodes to remove, I can look for vertex-disjoint paths between H and E. If I find 'k' vertex-disjoint paths, then at least 'k' nodes must be removed.

    Let's identify vertex-disjoint paths (paths that share no intermediate nodes):
    *   **Path 1:** H - C - B - E (Intermediate nodes: {C, B})
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - J - M - E (Intermediate nodes: {I, J, M})
    *   **Path 3:** H - G - F - P - E (Intermediate nodes: {G, F, P})

    Let's check for disjointness:
    *   Intermediate nodes of Path 1: {C, B}
    *   Intermediate nodes of Path 2: {I, J, M}
    *   Intermediate nodes of Path 3: {G, F, P}

    These three sets of intermediate nodes are completely disjoint. This means there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and E. Therefore, by Menger's Theorem, a minimum of 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect H and E.

5.  **Construct a Minimum Cut Set:** Since we know the minimum is 3, I need to find a set of 3 nodes that achieves this disconnection. Based on the groups identified in step 3:
    *   To cut Group 1 paths, remove **P**.
    *   To cut Group 2 paths, remove **B**.
    *   To cut Group 3 paths, remove one of {I, J, M}. Let's choose **J**.

    So, a potential cut set is [P, B, J].

6.  **Verify the Cut Set:**
    *   If **P** is removed: All paths in Group 1 are broken.
        Remaining paths: H - C - B - E and H - I - J - M - E.
    *   If **B** is also removed: The path H - C - B - E is broken.
        Remaining path: H - I - J - M - E.
    *   If **J** is also removed: The path H - I - J - M - E is broken.
        No paths remain between H and E.

    Thus, removing nodes P, B, and J successfully disconnects H and E. Since we established that at least 3 nodes are required, this set is minimal.

    A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all its incident edges.
    *   Node G has three incident edges: (G,F), (G,X), and (G,H).
    *   If we remove these three edges, G becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from G to I.

    *   Removing (G,F) breaks all paths starting with G-F (e.g., P3, P4, P6, P8, P9, P10).

    *   Removing (G,H) breaks all paths starting with G-H (e.g., P1, P2, P11).

    Since all paths from G to I must start with one of these three edges, removing them guarantees complete disconnection.

    *   Path 1 uses P.
    *   Path 2 uses C and B.

    *   **Path B:** H - I - J - M - O - N (Intermediate nodes: {I, J, M, O})

    *   Nodes in Path A: C
    *   Nodes in Path B: I, J, M, O
    *   Nodes in Path C: X, K, F, E, B

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B (black) and M (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect B and M.

1.  **Identify direct connections to M:**
    Node M is directly connected to nodes E, L, and J. This means any path from B to M must eventually pass through one of these three nodes and then use the edge connecting that node to M. The edges are (E, M), (L, M), and (J, M).

2.  **Consider a cut set by removing all edges incident to M:**
    If we remove the edges (E, M), (L, M), and (J, M), node M will have no remaining connections to the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between B and M. This forms a valid cut set of size 3.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut set (e.g., size 1 or 2) exists:**
    *   **Size 1:** It's clear that removing any single edge will not disconnect B and M, as there are multiple alternative paths. For example, if (E, M) is removed, paths like B-I-P-J-M or B-E-L-M still exist.
    *   **Size 2:** Let's try to find two edges whose removal would disconnect B and M.
        *   If we remove (E, M) and (L, M): Paths through J (e.g., B-I-P-J-M) would still connect B to M.
        *   If we remove (E, M) and (J, M): The path B-E-L-M would still connect B to M.
        *   If we remove (L, M) and (J, M): Paths through E (e.g., B-E-M) would still connect B to M.
        This shows that removing any two edges directly connected to M is not sufficient.

    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we can use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find three edge-disjoint paths from B to M:
    *   **Path 1:** B - E - M
        Edges: (B, E), (E, M)
    *   **Path 2:** B - I - P - J - M
        Edges: (B, I), (I, P), (P, J), (J, M)
    *   **Path 3:** B - O - P - E - L - M
        Edges: (B, O), (O, P), (P, E), (E, L), (L, M)

    Let's verify that these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint:
    *   Path 1 uses (B, E) and (E, M).
    *   Path 2 uses (B, I), (I, P), (P, J), (J, M). None of these edges are in Path 1.
    *   Path 3 uses (B, O), (O, P), (P, E), (E, L), (L, M). None of these edges are in Path 1 or Path 2.
    Since we have found three edge-disjoint paths between B and M, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them must be at least 3.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    We have identified a cut set of 3 edges [(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)] that disconnects B and M. We have also shown that there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths, meaning a cut of size 2 is not possible. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)]

These sets of intermediate nodes are mutually disjoint.

        *   J-I-C-H-G-F
        *   J-I-C-H-G-K-F
        *   J-I-C-N-B-E-F
        *   J-I-C-B-E-F

2.  **Consider a cut of size 1:**
    It's clear that removing any single edge will not disconnect J from F, as there are multiple independent routes. For example, removing (E,F) would still leave paths like J-I-H-X-F.

3.  **Consider a cut of size 2:**
    Let's try to find two edges that, if removed, would disconnect J from F.
    *   If we remove (E,F), all paths through E are cut. However, paths through I, H, X, G, K to F still exist (e.g., J-I-H-X-F). To cut these, we would need at least two more edges (e.g., (I,H) and (C,H) to cut off access to H from I and C). This would result in a total of 3 edges.
    *   If we remove (I,H) and (C,H), all paths from J to H via I or C are cut. However, all paths through M, P, D to E and then to F are still active (e.g., J-M-L-E-F). This would require at least one more edge (e.g., (E,F)). This would also result in a total of 3 edges.
    *   No combination of two edges seems to cut all paths. The graph has a high degree of connectivity and multiple parallel routes.

4.  **Consider a cut of size 3:**
    Let's try to find a set of 3 edges that disconnects J from F.
    A straightforward approach is to isolate one of the target nodes by removing all its outgoing/incoming edges to the rest of the graph.
    Node J has four direct connections: (J,D), (J,P), (J,M), (J,I).
    If we remove all four of these edges, J is completely isolated. This is a cut of size 4.

    Can we do better? Let's try to isolate J from the rest of the graph with fewer edges.
    Consider the edges: **(J,M), (J,P), (J,I)**.
    *   Removing **(J,M)** disconnects J from M, thus cutting all paths starting J-M.
    *   Removing **(J,P)** disconnects J from P, thus cutting all paths starting J-P.
    *   Removing **(J,I)** disconnects J from I, thus cutting all paths starting J-I.

    After removing these three edges, J is only connected to node D.
    Node D is connected to J and P. Since (J,P) has been removed, D is now only connected to J.
    Therefore, J is completely isolated from the rest of the graph (M, P, I, and all nodes reachable from them). This means J is disconnected from F.
    This cut set has a size of 3.

    Another valid cut set of size 3 is **{(E,F), (I,H), (C,H)}**:
    *   **(E,F)**: Cuts all paths that go through E to F (e.g., J-M-L-E-F, J-P-O-B-E-F, J-I-C-B-E-F).
    *   **(I,H)**: Cuts all paths from J-I directly to H (e.g., J-I-H-X-F, J-I-H-G-F).
    *   **(C,H)**: Cuts all paths from J-I-C to H (e.g., J-I-C-H-X-F, J-I-C-H-G-F).
    This set also disconnects J from F.

Since I have found a cut set of size 3, and I have systematically explored and confirmed that a cut of size 2 is not sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)]

6.  **Verify the minimum using Menger's Theorem (optional but good for confidence):**
    *   Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.
    *   Let's find edge-disjoint paths:
        *   Path A: L - M - J (Edges: (L, M), (M, J))
        *   Path B: L - E - P - J (Edges: (L, E), (E, P), (P, J))

    *   The maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is 2.
    *   Thus, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 3
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect E from J.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node E is connected to: L, M, F, G, B.
    *   Node J is connected to: M, K, I, P, D.

2.  **Identify all possible paths between E and J:**
    I will list a representative set of paths to understand the connectivity.
    *   **Path Group 1 (via M):**
        *   E - M - J
        *   E - L - M - J
    *   **Path Group 2 (via K):**
        *   E - F - K - J
        *   E - F - X - K - J
        *   E - G - F - K - J
    *   **Path Group 3 (via I):**
        *   E - G - H - I - J
        *   E - F - G - H - I - J
        *   E - B - C - I - J
        *   E - B - N - C - I - J
    *   **Path Group 4 (via P):**
        *   E - B - C - O - P - J
        *   E - B - N - C - O - P - J
        *   E - B - N - Y - O - P - J

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Edge-Connectivity):**
    Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges whose removal disconnects two non-adjacent nodes (the edge-connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. If I can find N edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size is N.

    Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths between E and J:
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J
        *   Edges: (E, M), (M, J)
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - K - J
        *   Edges: (E, F), (F, K), (K, J)
    *   **Path 3:** E - G - H - I - J
        *   Edges: (E, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)
    *   **Path 4:** E - B - C - O - P - J
        *   Edges: (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)

    Let's verify if these four paths are edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
    *   Edges in Path 1: (E, M), (M, J)
    *   Edges in Path 2: (E, F), (F, K), (K, J)
    *   Edges in Path 3: (E, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)
    *   Edges in Path 4: (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)

    Upon inspection, all edges listed across these four paths are distinct. No edge is used in more than one path. Therefore, these are 4 edge-disjoint paths between E and J.

4.  **Determine the minimum number of edges to remove:**
    Since there are 4 edge-disjoint paths, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect E from J is 4.

5.  **Identify a minimal cut set:**
    To disconnect E from J, we need to cut at least one edge from each of these 4 edge-disjoint paths. A straightforward way to do this is to cut the edges directly connected to J that are part of these paths.
    *   Cut (M, J) to break Path 1.
    *   Cut (K, J) to break Path 2.
    *   Cut (I, J) to break Path 3.
    *   Cut (P, J) to break Path 4.

    This set of edges [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)] has 4 edges. Removing these edges ensures that no path exists from E to J. The edge (J, D) does not need to be removed as D is not connected to E's side of the graph.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)]

- H is connected to I, G, X.
- B is connected to E, N, C, O.

Next, I consider all possible paths between H and B. I can categorize these paths into two main groups based on the initial connections from H and the final connections to B:

Group 1: Paths starting from H through I (the "left" side of the graph) and ending at B.
- H - I - C - B
- H - I - C - N - B
- H - I - C - O - B
- H - I - P - O - B
- H - I - P - C - B

Group 2: Paths starting from H through G or X (the "bottom" side of the graph) and ending at B.
- H - G - X - F - E - B
- H - G - K - F - E - B
- H - G - F - E - B
- H - X - F - E - B
- H - X - K - F - E - B
- H - X - G - F - E - B

1.  **Can a single edge disconnect H and B?**
    If I remove any single edge, there will always be an alternative path. For example, removing (H, I) would cut all paths in Group 1, but all paths in Group 2 would remain. Removing (E, B) would cut all paths in Group 2, but all paths in Group 1 would remain. Therefore, a single edge is not sufficient. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

2.  **Can two edges disconnect H and B?**
    I need to find two edges such that their removal breaks all paths in both Group 1 and Group 2.

    Let's consider cutting one edge that severs all paths in Group 1, and one edge that severs all paths in Group 2.

    *   **To cut all paths in Group 1 (H -> I -> ... -> B):**
        The most direct way to cut all paths originating from H via I is to remove the edge (H, I). If (H, I) is removed, H can no longer reach I, and thus cannot reach B through any of the paths in Group 1.

    *   **To cut all paths in Group 2 (H -> G/X -> ... -> E -> B):**
        All paths in Group 2 eventually converge and pass through the node F, and then through E, to reach B. Specifically, all these paths end with the segment F - E - B. Therefore, removing the edge (E, B) would effectively cut all paths in Group 2.

    Let's test the cut set: **[(H, I), (E, B)]**.

    *   **Step 1: Remove (H, I).**
        H is now only connected to G and X. All paths that started H-I-... are broken.

    *   **Step 2: Remove (E, B).**
        B is now only connected to N, C, O. All paths that ended ...-E-B are broken.

    Now, I need to verify if any path still exists from H to B.
    After removing (H, I), H can only reach G or X.
    After removing (E, B), B can only be reached from N, C, or O.

    Can G or X reach N, C, or O?
    - G is connected to X, K, F.
    - X is connected to G, K, F.
    - K is connected to X, G, F.
    - F is connected to K, G, X (and E, but (E,B) is removed, so F cannot reach B via E).

    None of the nodes G, X, K, or F are directly connected to N, C, or O. Furthermore, there are no indirect paths from {G, X, K, F} to {N, C, O} without passing through E (which is now disconnected from B) or I (which is disconnected from H). Therefore, no path exists between H and B after removing (H, I) and (E, B).

This cut set of 2 edges successfully disconnects H and B. Since I've already established that 1 edge is not enough, 2 edges is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(H, I), (E, B)]

    *   A simple way to disconnect a node from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges incident to it. If we remove all edges incident to D, it will be isolated. This would require removing 2 edges: (D, J) and (D, P). This gives an upper bound for the minimum cut size of 2.

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths:**
    According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes (s-t edge cut) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes. If I can find two edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size must be at least 2.

    Let's find two edge-disjoint paths from D to F:
    *   **Path 1:** D - J - I - H - X - F
        Edges in Path 1: (D, J), (J, I), (I, H), (H, X), (X, F)

        Edges in Path 2: (D, P), (P, O), (O, N), (N, B), (B, F)

    Upon inspection, these two paths share no common edges. They are completely edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut size:**
    *   Since there are at least two edge-disjoint paths between D and F, we must remove at least 2 edges to disconnect them.
    *   We identified a cut set of size 2 by removing the edges directly connected to D: [(D, J), (D, P)].

5.  **Verify the chosen cut set:**
    *   If edges (D, J) and (D, P) are removed, node D becomes an isolated node.
    *   With D isolated, no path can originate from D and reach any other node in the graph, including F.
    *   Therefore, removing these two edges successfully disconnects D from F.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    The minimum number of edges to remove is 2, as confirmed by both the degree of node D and the existence of two edge-disjoint paths.

1.  **Path 1: H - I - O**
    *   Intermediate node: I

2.  **Path 2: H - B - C - O**
    *   Intermediate nodes: B, C

    *   Intermediate nodes: F, M, J, P
    *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (I) and Path 2 (B, C).

I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between H and O. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect H and O is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3 and verify that its removal disconnects H and O. I will choose one node from each of the identified vertex-disjoint paths (excluding H and O).
Let's choose:
*   From Path 1: **I**
*   From Path 2: **B** (or C)
*   From Path 3: **J** (or F, M, P)

So, my proposed cut set is **{I, B, J}**.

Now, I will systematically verify that removing these three nodes (I, B, J) breaks all possible paths from H to O. I will consider all possible first steps from H:

Image name: POGBa_task3_gt2_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 3
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and D (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from D.

1.  **Identify direct connections to target nodes:**
    *   Node E is connected to F, M, L, B.
    *   Node D is connected to J, P, Y.

2.  **Consider the "exit" points from D:**
    Any path from E to D must eventually pass through one of D's direct neighbors (J, P, or Y) and then use the edge connecting that neighbor to D.
    Therefore, removing the edges (J, D), (P, D), and (Y, D) would completely isolate D from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from E. This is a cut set of size 3.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., size 2) is possible:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I can use Menger's theorem, which states that the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between two non-adjacent nodes is equal to the minimum number of vertices that must be removed to disconnect them (vertex connectivity). Similarly, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is equal to the minimum number of edges that must be removed (edge connectivity).

    Let's find the minimum vertex cut between E and D (excluding E and D themselves).
    Consider the nodes J, P, Y. These are the only nodes directly connected to D.
    *   If we remove only J, paths like E-...-P-D and E-...-Y-D still exist.
    *   If we remove only P, paths like E-...-J-D and E-...-Y-D still exist.
    *   If we remove only Y, paths like E-...-J-D and E-...-P-D still exist.
    *   If we remove any two of {J, P, Y} (e.g., J and P), the third node (Y) still provides a path to D (E-...-Y-D).
    *   If we remove all three nodes {J, P, Y}, then D becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph.
    Therefore, the minimum vertex cut separating E and D is 3 (the set {J, P, Y}).
    Since the minimum vertex cut is 3, the minimum edge cut must also be 3.

4.  **Find a minimal cut set of 3 edges:**
    Based on the above, one valid cut set of 3 edges is to remove all edges directly connected to D:
    Cut Set 1: [(J, D), (P, D), (Y, D)]

    Let's try to find another cut set of 3 edges by considering the "entry points" from E's side of the graph. All paths from E to D must start with one of E's direct connections: (E, F), (E, M), (E, L), or (E, B).
    *   **Paths starting E-B**: All paths that begin with E-B (e.g., E-B-H-I-J-D, E-B-C-O-Y-D, etc.) can be cut by removing the edge (E, B).
    *   **Paths starting E-F**: All paths that begin with E-F (e.g., E-F-G-H-I-J-D, E-F-X-G-H-I-P-D, etc.) can be cut by removing the edge (E, F).
    *   **Paths starting E-M or E-L**:
        *   E-M-J-D: This path exists.
        *   E-L-M-J-D: This path also exists.
        All paths from E that go through M (or L, which leads to M) must eventually pass through J to reach D, as M is only connected to E, L, and J. Therefore, removing the edge (M, J) will cut all paths from E that go through M.

    So, the cut set [(E, B), (E, F), (M, J)] consists of 3 edges.
    Let's verify this cut set:
    *   Removing (E, B) disconnects all paths from E that go through B.
    *   Removing (E, F) disconnects all paths from E that go through F.
    *   Removing (M, J) disconnects all paths from E that go through M (or L then M) to J and then to D.
    Since all paths from E must start with E-B, E-F, or E-M (or E-L, which leads to M), this set of 3 edges successfully disconnects E from D.

Both identified cut sets have a size of 3, which matches the minimum determined by Menger's theorem.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, B), (E, F), (M, J)]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B and M. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect B from M. This is a min-cut problem. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to the target node M:**
    Node M is connected to E, J, and L. Therefore, any path from B to M must traverse one of these three edges: (E, M), (J, M), or (L, M).

2.  **Consider a simple cut set:**
    If we remove all edges directly connected to M, M will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from B. This set would be [(E, M), (J, M), (L, M)]. This cut set has 3 edges.

3.  **Attempt to find a smaller cut set (e.g., 2 edges):**
    Let's assume a cut set of 2 edges exists.
    *   If we remove any two of the edges directly connected to M, say (E, M) and (J, M), there would still be a path through L (e.g., B - H - E - L - M). This path remains active because (L, M) and (E, L) are not removed.
    *   Similarly, removing (E, M) and (L, M) would leave paths through J (e.g., B - I - J - M).
    *   Removing (J, M) and (L, M) would leave paths through E (e.g., B - H - E - M).
    This demonstrates that removing any two edges directly connected to M is not sufficient to disconnect B from M.
    Therefore, if a cut of size 2 exists, at least one of the edges must be "upstream" from M's direct neighbors (E, J, L).

4.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we can try to find three edge-disjoint paths from B to M. If we find three such paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum cut must be at least 3.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** B - H - E - M
        Edges: (B, H), (H, E), (E, M)

    *   **Path 2:** B - I - J - M
        Edges: (B, I), (I, J), (J, M)

    *   **Path 3:** B - C - I - E - L - M
        Edges: (B, C), (C, I), (I, E), (E, L), (L, M)

    Let's verify that these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
    *   Edges in Path 1: {(B, H), (H, E), (E, M)}
    *   Edges in Path 2: {(B, I), (I, J), (J, M)}
    *   Edges in Path 3: {(B, C), (C, I), (I, E), (E, L), (L, M)}

    Comparing the sets of edges, there are no common edges between any two of these three paths. Therefore, these are three edge-disjoint paths from B to M.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since there are at least three edge-disjoint paths from B to M, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect B from M is at least 3. We have already identified a cut set of 3 edges: [(E, M), (J, M), (L, M)]. This set successfully disconnects M from the rest of the graph. Since we found a cut set of size 3 and proved that the minimum cut must be at least 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Image name: PGS_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 3
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and H (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E and H.

First, I will try to determine the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H. By Menger's theorem, this number will be equal to the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them.

Let's identify edge-disjoint paths:
1.  **Path 1: E - B - H**
    Edges used: `(E, B)`, `(B, H)`

2.  **Path 2: E - F - G - H**
    Edges used: `(E, F)`, `(F, G)`, `(G, H)`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it uses entirely different edges.

Now, I need to check if a third edge-disjoint path exists. A third path must not use any of the edges from Path 1 or Path 2: `(E, B)`, `(B, H)`, `(E, F)`, `(F, G)`, `(G, H)`.

Let's examine the remaining connections from E and to H:
*   Edges available from E: `(E, L)`, `(E, M)`
*   Edges available to H: `(H, I)` (since `(B, H)` and `(G, H)` are used in P1 and P2 respectively)

If a third edge-disjoint path exists, it must start with `(E, L)` or `(E, M)` and end with `(I, H)`. This means we need to find a path from E to I that does not use any of the forbidden edges.

Let's trace paths from E to I, avoiding forbidden edges:
*   **Starting with E - L:**
    *   E - L - M. (`(E, L)` and `(L, M)` are available).
    *   From M, the only connection is to F. (M, F) is available.
    *   E - L - M - F. From F, `(F, G)` is forbidden. `(F, X)` and `(F, K)` are available.
    *   E - L - M - F - X. From X, `(X, G)` and `(X, K)` are available.
    *   E - L - M - F - K. From K, `(K, G)` is available.
    *   Nodes L, M, F, X, K are not connected to any of C, I, J, P, D, O, N, Y. Therefore, no path from E starting with `(E, L)` can reach I.

*   **Starting with E - M:**
    *   E - M - L. (This leads to the same set of nodes as E - L - M, and cannot reach I).
    *   E - M - F. (Similar to E - L - M - F, leads to X, K, G, but not I).
    *   Nodes M, F, X, K are not connected to any of C, I, J, P, D, O, N, Y. Therefore, no path from E starting with `(E, M)` can reach I.

This analysis shows that all paths from E to I must pass through node B. Since the edge `(E, B)` is already used in Path 1, there is no third path from E to I that is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

Therefore, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H is 2. By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E and H is 2.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 2 edges that achieves this disconnection.
Let's consider the edges `(E, B)` and `(G, H)`.

1.  **Removing (E, B):**
    *   This edge directly connects E to B. Its removal disconnects E from B.
    *   Since B is the only node through which E can reach the subgraph containing C, N, I, J, P, D, O, Y, removing `(E, B)` effectively cuts off all paths from E to H that pass through B or I (e.g., E-B-H, E-B-C-I-H, etc.).

2.  **Removing (G, H):**
    *   This edge directly connects G to H. Its removal disconnects H from G.
    *   All paths from E to H that pass through G (e.g., E-F-G-H, E-F-X-G-H, E-F-K-G-H, E-M-F-G-H, etc.) are now broken because G cannot reach H.

With both `(E, B)` and `(G, H)` removed, all paths from E to H are broken.
*   Paths through B or I are cut by `(E, B)`.
*   Paths through G are cut by `(G, H)`.
Since H is only connected to B, G, and I, these two edge removals ensure no path exists between E and H.

To confirm that 2 is the minimum, we must show that removing any single edge is not sufficient.
*   If `(E, B)` is removed, paths like E-F-G-H still exist.
*   If `(G, H)` is removed, paths like E-B-H still exist.
*   If `(B, H)` is removed, paths like E-B-C-I-H and E-F-G-H still exist.
*   If any other single edge is removed, it's easy to find a path between E and H.

Thus, 2 is indeed the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, B), (G, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---

4.  **Identify critical connections:**
    From the analysis above, it's clear that any path from H to N must eventually pass through either node B or node I. The connections from H to G (H-G) lead to a sub-graph (G, F, K, X) that can only connect back to the main graph (and thus to N) via node E, which then connects to B and I. Therefore, all paths from H to N must ultimately traverse either the edge (H, B) or the edge (H, I), or both (if there's a path like H-G-F-E-B-N, it still relies on B being reachable from H, which it is via (H,B) or (H,G-F-E-B)).

5.  **Determine a minimal cut set:**
    *   **Can we disconnect with 1 edge?** No. For example, removing (H, B) would break paths like H-B-N, but paths like H-I-C-N would still exist. Similarly, removing (H, I) would leave paths through B. Removing any single edge directly connected to N (e.g., (N, B)) would still leave other paths to N (e.g., H-I-C-N, H-I-P-O-N). Therefore, at least 2 edges must be removed.

    *   **Can we disconnect with 2 edges?**
        Consider removing the edges (H, B) and (H, I).
        *   If (H, B) is removed, H can no longer directly reach B.
        *   If (H, I) is removed, H can no longer directly reach I.
        *   Now, H is only connected to G.
        *   From G, the only way to reach the part of the graph containing N is via G-F-E. However, E is connected to B and I. Since H can no longer reach B or I (due to the removal of (H, B) and (H, I)), any path like H-G-F-E-B-...-N or H-G-F-E-I-...-N is effectively broken from H's perspective.
        *   Therefore, removing (H, B) and (H, I) completely disconnects H from all possible paths leading to N.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since a single edge removal is insufficient, and we found a set of 2 edges that disconnects H and N, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(H, B), (H, I)]

    *   Therefore, P1, P2, and P3 are three distinct edge-disjoint paths between B and H.

7.  **Identify a Minimal Cut Set:** To disconnect H from B, we can remove all edges directly connected to H.

    *   Let's propose the Cut Set: [(H, C), (H, I), (H, G)].

    *   Since we found a cut set of size 3, and we previously determined that at least 3 edges are required, this is a minimal cut set.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, C), (H, I), (H, G)]

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are G and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect G from N. I cannot remove G or N.

First, I identify all possible paths from G to N.
Let's list the direct neighbors of G: H, F, K.
Let's list the direct neighbors of N: B, C, Y.

Any path from G to N must pass through one of G's neighbors and eventually reach one of N's neighbors.

Let's analyze the connections to N:
*   N is connected to B.
*   N is connected to C.
*   N is connected to Y.

Now let's look at Y. Y is only connected to N and C. This means any path from G that reaches Y must have come through C. Therefore, if C is removed, Y becomes isolated from the rest of the graph (except N), and no path from G can reach N via Y.

This implies that all paths from G to N must ultimately pass through either node B or node C.

Let's test this hypothesis by considering paths:
1.  **Paths through H (from G):**
    *   G-H-B-N (passes through B)
    *   G-H-I-C-N (passes through C)
    *   G-H-I-P-O-C-N (passes through C)
    *   G-H-I-P-J-M-L-E-B-N (passes through B)

2.  **Paths through F (from G):**
    *   G-F-E-B-N (passes through B)
    *   G-F-M-L-E-B-N (passes through B)
    *   G-F-M-J-P-O-C-N (passes through C)

3.  **Paths through K (from G):**
    *   G-K-X-F-E-B-N (passes through B)
    *   G-K-X-F-M-L-E-B-N (passes through B)
    *   G-K-X-F-M-J-P-O-C-N (passes through C)

Indeed, every path from G to N passes through either B or C.

Now, let's determine the minimum number of nodes to remove:

*   **Can we disconnect G and N by removing only one node?**
    *   If we remove B: Paths through C (e.g., G-H-I-C-N) would still exist. So, removing B alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove C: Paths through B (e.g., G-H-B-N) would still exist. So, removing C alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove Y: Y is only connected to N and C. Removing Y would not affect paths through B or C directly to N. For example, G-H-B-N and G-H-I-C-N would still exist. So, removing Y alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove any other single node (e.g., H, I, P, O, E, F, M, L, J, X, K, D), there would always be alternative paths through B or C. For example, removing H would leave paths like G-F-E-B-N or G-F-M-J-P-O-C-N.

*   **Can we disconnect G and N by removing two nodes?**
    *   Since all paths must pass through either B or C, removing both B and C would sever all connections from G to N.
    *   If B is removed, all paths that used B are broken.
    *   If C is removed, all paths that used C (including those that might have gone through Y) are broken.
    *   After removing B and C, N is only connected to Y. However, Y is also only connected to N (since C is removed). Thus, N and Y form an isolated component, and G cannot reach N.

Therefore, removing nodes B and C will disconnect G from N. Since a single node removal is insufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [B, C]

The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, O and H. This is a minimum edge cut problem. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

First, let's identify the neighbors of the target nodes:
Neighbors of O: P, Y, N, M
Neighbors of H: I, C, X, G

Next, I will attempt to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between O and H. Edge-disjoint means no two paths share any common edges.

1.  **Path 1 (ending at I):**
    O - M - J - I - H
    Edges used: {(O, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)}

2.  **Path 2 (ending at C, avoiding edges from Path 1):**
    O - P - Y - N - C - H
    Edges used: {(O, P), (P, Y), (Y, N), (N, C), (C, H)}
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

3.  **Path 3 (ending at X, avoiding edges from Path 1 and Path 2):**
    O - N - B - E - F - X - H
    Edges used: {(O, N), (N, B), (B, E), (E, F), (F, X), (X, H)}
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

Edges connected to O are (O, M), (O, P), (O, N), (O, Y).
From the paths identified:
*   (O, M) is used by Path 1.
*   (O, P) is used by Path 2.
*   (O, N) is used by Path 3.

O - Y
From Y, the available edges are (Y, P) and (Y, N).
*   (Y, P) is used by Path 2.
*   (Y, N) is used by Path 2.
Since both outgoing edges from Y are already used by Path 2, no fourth edge-disjoint path can start with (O, Y). This means there are no more edge-disjoint paths from O to H.

Therefore, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between O and H is 3. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect O and H is 3.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that achieves this disconnection. I will look for edges that act as "bottlenecks" for the different groups of paths.

Consider the following set of 3 edges: [(J, I), (N, C), (E, F)].

    *   O - M - J - I - H (Cut by (J, I))
    *   O - M - L - E - B - N - Y - P - D - J - I - H (Cut by (J, I))

    *   O - N - C - H (Cut by (N, C))
    *   O - N - B - C - H (Cut by (N, C))
    *   O - M - L - E - B - N - C - H (Cut by (N, C))

3.  **Remove (E, F):** This edge cuts all paths that pass through E and then F to reach H (via X or G).
    *   O - N - B - E - F - X - H (Cut by (E, F))
    *   O - N - B - E - F - K - X - H (Cut by (E, F))
    *   O - N - B - E - F - G - H (Cut by (E, F))

    None of these nodes are the target node B.
*   **Level 2:** Neighbors of nodes from Level 1 (excluding already visited nodes)
    *   From L: L is connected to E. Path: [M, L, E]
    *   From E: E is connected to L (visited), P. Path: [M, E, P]. E is also connected to B. Path: [M, E, B].
    *   From J: J is connected to M (visited), P. Path: [M, J, P]. J is connected to D. Path: [M, J, D].

At Level 2, I found a path to the target node B: [M, E, B]. This path has a length of 2. Since BFS explores level by level, the first path found to the target node is guaranteed to be the shortest. A path of length 1 would mean M and B are directly connected, which they are not. Therefore, a path of length 2 is the shortest possible.

**Verification of path [M, E, B]:**
1.  Is M directly connected to E? Yes, there is a continuous black line between M and E.
2.  Is E directly connected to B? Yes, there is a continuous black line between E and B.

The path is valid and has a length of 2.

Final Answer: Length: 2, Path: [M, E, B]
